\textbf{Extending the table diagonally.} To find $P(n+1)$: since $\Delta^nP(1)=c$ too (the $n$-th difference is constant everywhere), start a new top-to-bottom diagonal with $\Delta^nP(1)=c$ and fill it downward using $\Delta^{j-1}P(k+1)=\Delta^{j-1}P(k)+\Delta^jP(k)$ — each new diagonal entry is the sum of the entry above-left of it and the entry directly above it, both already known from the existing table. After $n$ such steps the diagonal reaches $\Delta^0P(n+1)=P(n+1)$, using $O(n)$ arithmetic operations.

\textbf{Iterating.} Repeat this once for each of $P(n+1),\dots,P(2n)$: to get $P(n+1+t)$, extend a new diagonal starting from $\Delta^nP(1+t)=c$ (still the same constant), using only previously computed table entries, again in $O(n)$ operations. After $n$ repetitions, all of $P(n+1),\dots,P(2n)$ are known.

\textbf{Complexity.} Building the initial table costs $O(n^2)$; each of the $n$ extrapolation steps costs $O(n)$, for another $O(n^2)$. Total time and space are both
\[
\boxed{O(n^2)}.
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Finding one of $n$ hidden points in a half-disk via nearest-distance queries]
A half-disk (semicircular region) hides $n$ unknown points. You may repeatedly pick any point $X$ and ask ``what is the distance from $X$ to the nearest hidden point?'' (a distance of $0$ means $X$ itself is a hidden point). Prove that at least one hidden point can always be found using at most $2n+1$ queries.
\end{example}
\begin{proof}[Solution]
\textbf{Setup.} Query $n+1$ points $A_1<A_2<\cdots<A_{n+1}$, placed in this order along the diameter of the half-disk, recording the returned distances $r_1,\dots,r_{n+1}$. For each $i$, let $f(i)$ denote which of the $n$ hidden points is nearest to $A_i$ (well-defined a.s.; ties are a measure-zero degeneracy that can be perturbed away).

\textbf{Key structural fact.} The Voronoi cell of any hidden point $Z$ (the set of locations closer to $Z$ than to any other hidden point) is convex, being an intersection of half-planes. The intersection of a convex planar region with a straight line (here, the diameter) is a single connected sub-interval. Consequently, if $f(i)=f(j)$ for some $i<j$ (both $A_i,A_j$ nearest to the same hidden point $Z$), then $A_i$ and $A_j$ both lie in $Z$'s Voronoi interval on the diameter, so every $A_k$ with $i\le k\le j$ — lying between them on the line — lies in that same interval too, giving $f(k)=Z$ for all such $k$. In particular $f(i)=f(i+1)$.

\textbf{Pigeonhole.} The map $f$ sends $n+1$ indices into a set of $n$ hidden points, so some value repeats: $f(i)=f(j)$ for some $i<j$. By the structural fact just proved, this forces $f(i^*)=f(i^*+1)$ for some \emph{adjacent} pair $i^*\in\{1,\dots,n\}$.

\textbf{Locating the shared point.} For that adjacent pair, the hidden point $Z=f(i^*)$ is at distance exactly $r_{i^*}$ from $A_{i^*}$ and exactly $r_{i^*+1}$ from $A_{i^*+1}$ (being the nearest point to each). So $Z$ lies on both the circle of radius $r_{i^*}$ centered at $A_{i^*}$ and the circle of radius $r_{i^*+1}$ centered at $A_{i^*+1}$. Two such circles (centered at distinct points of the diameter) intersect in at most $2$ points, symmetric about the diameter line; since all hidden points lie strictly within the half-disk, only the intersection point on the half-disk's side (if any) is a valid candidate — the mirror point across the diameter can be discarded without a query.

\textbf{The algorithm.} For each of the $n$ adjacent pairs $(A_i,A_{i+1})$, $i=1,\dots,n$, compute the circle intersection on the half-disk side (if the two circles intersect at all) and query that single candidate point — $1$ query per pair, $n$ queries total. Since we don't know in advance which pair is the true matching pair $i^*$, we simply try all $n$; the pair $i^*$ guaranteed to exist by pigeonhole will, when its turn comes, return distance $0$ (its candidate point coincides with $Z$ itself), successfully identifying a hidden point.

\textbf{Total.} $n+1$ queries for the initial sweep, plus at most $n$ queries for the adjacent-pair candidates:
\[
\boxed{(n+1)+n=2n+1\text{ queries suffice}}.
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Two non-attacking matrix elements with maximum product]
An $n\times n$ matrix is filled with distinct positive integers. Design an algorithm to find two entries, no two in the same row or column, whose product is as large as possible, in $O(n^2)$ time and $O(n)$ extra memory.
\end{example}
\begin{proof}[Solution]
Let $a$ be the largest entry in the matrix and $b$ the second largest (found by one $O(n^2)$ scan, tracking values and positions).

\textbf{If $a,b$ are compatible} (different row and column), the answer is simply $a\cdot b$ — unbeatable, since these are literally the two largest entries in the whole matrix.

\textbf{Key lemma: if $a,b$ conflict (share a row or column), the optimal pair always includes $a$ or $b$.} Suppose $a,b$ share row $R$ (the column case is symmetric), with $a$ at column $c_a\ne c_b$, $b$'s column. Suppose toward contradiction that an optimal compatible pair $(x,y)$ avoids both $a$ and $b$; then $x,y<a$ and, since $b$ is the \emph{second}-largest entry overall, also $x,y<b$ (as $x,y\ne a,b$). If $x$ were compatible with $a$ (different row/column), then $a\cdot x>b\cdot x\ge y\cdot x$ — wait, more directly: $a\cdot x>y\cdot x$ since $a>y$ (as $y\ne a$), contradicting that $(x,y)$ beats every pair through $a$. So $x$ must conflict with $a$; likewise, if $x$ were compatible with $b$, then $b\cdot x>y\cdot x$ (since $b>y$), again a contradiction — so $x$ must conflict with $b$ too. The same argument applied to $y$ (using $a>x$ and $b>x$) shows $y$ conflicts with both $a$ and $b$ as well.

Now, conflicting with \emph{both} $a$ and $b$: since $a,b$ have different columns ($c_a\ne c_b$), no single element can match both columns at once, so any element conflicting with both $a$ and $b$ must instead share their common row $R$. Hence $x$ and $y$ are \emph{both} in row $R$ — but then $x,y$ share a row, contradicting that $(x,y)$ is itself a compatible pair. This contradiction proves the lemma.

\textbf{Algorithm.} If $a,b$ conflict: compute $a^*=$ the maximum entry outside $a$'s row and column, and $b^*=$ the maximum entry outside $b$'s row and column (two more $O(n^2)$ scans). By the lemma, the optimal answer is
\[
\boxed{\max(a\cdot a^*,\ b\cdot b^*)}
\]
(with $a\cdot b$ handled separately when $a,b$ are already compatible). This uses $O(n^2)$ time (three linear passes over the matrix) and $O(1)\subset O(n)$ extra memory (a handful of running maxima and their positions).
\end{proof}

\section*{How to Use This Guide Efficiently}
\begin{example}[Guessing a number faster than the naive doubling bound against one lie]
A number from $1$ to $n$ ($n>10$) must be guessed using yes/no questions, where the answerer may lie at most once. Design a strategy that guarantees success in strictly fewer than $2\lceil\log_2n\rceil+1$ questions.
\end{example}
\begin{proof}[Solution]
\textbf{Reduction to error-correcting codes.} Let $k=\lceil\log_2n\rceil$, so the target number's index has a $k$-bit binary representation $b_1,\dots,b_k$. Instead of the naive scheme (ask each bit, then ask all $k$ again to verify — $2k$ questions), encode the $k$-bit string using a binary code with \emph{minimum Hamming distance $3$}: a set of $n$ codewords of some length $q=k+r$ such that any two differ in at least $3$ positions. Ask, as yes/no questions, the $q$ bits of the target's codeword (each such question — ``does bit $i$ of the codeword of your secret number equal $1$?'' — is a legitimate subset-membership question about the number). With at most one lie, the received length-$q$ string differs from the true codeword in at most $1$ position; since any two codewords differ in $\ge3$ positions, the true codeword is the \emph{unique} codeword within Hamming distance $1$ of what was received (two codewords within distance $1$ of the same string would be within distance $2$ of each other, contradicting minimum distance $3$) — so nearest-codeword decoding always recovers the number correctly.

\textbf{Building such a code (Hamming code).} Take $q=k+r$ where $r$ is the smallest integer with $2^r\ge q+1=k+r+1$ (the classical Hamming-code parameter relation). Index the $q$ coordinate positions by the nonzero vectors of $\mathbb F_2^r$ (there are $2^r-1\ge k+r=q$ of them, enough labels). Designate $r$ of the positions — those indexed by the $r$ standard basis vectors — as \emph{parity-check} bits, and the remaining $k$ positions as \emph{data} bits, holding $b_1,\dots,b_k$ directly. Set each parity-check bit at position $e_j$ to the XOR of all data bits whose index vector has a $1$ in coordinate $j$ (a legitimate yes/no question: ``is the XOR of these specific bits of your number's index equal to $1$?''). This is exactly the (shortened) binary Hamming code, whose minimum distance is $3$: no codeword has weight $1$ (a lone data-bit error would flip a parity computed from it, so it's inconsistent with weight $\le1$ being a valid codeword difference) or weight $2$ (two flipped positions with distinct nonzero index vectors sum to a nonzero vector, again violating consistency of the parity equations) — the standard argument for why the columns of the parity-check matrix, being all distinct and nonzero, forbid low-weight codewords.

\textbf{Question count.} $q=k+r$ where $2^r\ge k+r+1$. Since $n>10$ gives $k=\lceil\log_2n\rceil\ge4$, and $r$ grows only logarithmically in $k$ (e.g.\ $k=4\Rightarrow r=3$ suffices, since $2^3=8\ge4+3+1=8$; larger $k$ needs even smaller $r/k$ ratio), we always have $r\le k$ once $k\ge4$, so
\[
q=k+r\le2k<2k+1=2\lceil\log_2n\rceil+1.
\]
\[
\boxed{q=\lceil\log_2n\rceil+r\text{ questions suffice, strictly fewer than }2\lceil\log_2n\rceil+1}
\]
(with $r=O(\log\log n)$, so the true savings grow — the scheme beats the naive bound by an increasing margin as $n$ grows, though pinning down the information-theoretically \emph{optimal} question count for this classical Rényi–Ulam ``liar'' problem requires a more delicate adaptive argument beyond what's needed here).
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Finding the $i$-th digit of a concatenation-recurrence sequence in $O(1)$ memory]
Define $a_n$ (for $n\ge3$) by writing $a_{n-2}$ immediately after $a_{n-1}$ (decimal concatenation), starting from arbitrary naturals $a_1,a_2$. Given $n$ and $i$, design an algorithm to find the $i$-th digit of $a_n$ using $O(1)$ extra memory (a constant number of stored numbers), and analyze its time complexity.
\end{example}
\begin{proof}[Solution]
\textbf{Digit-length recursion.} Let $L_k$ be the number of digits of $a_k$. Since $a_k$ is $a_{k-1}$ followed by $a_{k-2}$, $L_k=L_{k-1}+L_{k-2}$ — a Fibonacci-type recursion, computable from the (given, known-length) $L_1,L_2$.

\textbf{Locating the digit recursively.} The $i$-th digit of $a_k$ (for $k\ge3$) is: the $i$-th digit of $a_{k-1}$, if $i\le L_{k-1}$ (it falls within the first, $a_{k-1}$, part); otherwise the $(i-L_{k-1})$-th digit of $a_{k-2}$. Repeatedly applying this reduces $(k,i)$ down to a base case $k\in\{1,2\}$, where the answer is read directly off the given number $a_1$ or $a_2$.

\textbf{Avoiding $O(n)$ memory.} Naively this recursion needs $L_{k-1}$ at every level, which would mean storing the whole array $L_1,\dots,L_n$ ($O(n)$ numbers) to avoid recomputation. Instead, maintain only the \emph{current pair} $(P,Q)=(L_{m-1},L_{m-2})$ for the current level $m$, and update it as $m$ decreases:
\begin{itemize}
\item Reducing from level $m$ to level $m-1$ needs the pair $(L_{m-2},L_{m-3})$. We already know $L_{m-2}=Q$, and $L_{m-3}=L_{m-1}-L_{m-2}=P-Q$ (rearranging the defining recursion) — so the new pair is $(Q,\,P-Q)$, computed with a single subtraction, no lookup needed.
\item Reducing from level $m$ to level $m-2$ (the ``otherwise'' branch, which skips level $m-1$ entirely) is just \emph{two} applications of the same update.
\end{itemize}
So only the pair $(P,Q)$, the current level $m$, and the current index $i$ need to be stored — $O(1)$ numbers throughout (each of arbitrary size, per the usual convention for such problems, since $L_n$ itself grows exponentially in $n$).

\textbf{Algorithm.} (1) Build the initial pair $(L_{n-1},L_{n-2})$ by iterating the Fibonacci-style recursion \emph{forward} from $(L_1,L_2)$ up to index $n$, keeping only the two most recent values at each step. (2) Walk the reduction \emph{backward} as described, updating $(P,Q)$ and $(m,i)$ at each step, until $m\in\{1,2\}$. (3) Return the $i$-th digit of the given $a_1$ or $a_2$.

\textbf{Complexity.} Step (1) performs $O(n)$ additions; step (2) performs $O(n)$ subtractions (the level $m$ decreases by $1$ or $2$ each time, so at most $n$ iterations). Each individual arithmetic operation involves numbers with $\Theta(k)$ digits at level $k$ (Fibonacci-like growth), so
\[
\boxed{O(n)\text{ arithmetic operations }(O(n^2)\text{ bit operations}),\ O(1)\text{ stored numbers}}.
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Finding a social-network celebrity in $n$ queries]
A social network has $n$ users; ``$A$ follows $B$'' need not be symmetric. It is known that exactly one user (the celebrity) is followed by all other $n-1$ users and follows no one. Using queries of the form ``does $A$ follow $B$?'', find the celebrity in at most $n$ queries.
\end{example}
\begin{proof}[Solution]
\textbf{Key elimination fact.} For any two users $A\ne B$: if $A$ follows $B$, then $A$ cannot be the celebrity (the celebrity follows no one); if $A$ does \emph{not} follow $B$, then $B$ cannot be the celebrity (everyone follows the celebrity, so $B$'s failure to be followed by $A$ disqualifies $B$). So a single query about any pair always eliminates exactly one of the two from contention.

\textbf{Algorithm.} Maintain a single candidate, initialized to user $1$. For $l=2,\dots,n$, query whether the current candidate follows user $l$: if yes, the candidate is disqualified and $l$ becomes the new candidate; if no, $l$ is disqualified and the candidate is unchanged. This uses exactly $n-1$ queries.

\textbf{Correctness.} Let $C$ be the true celebrity. Whenever the current candidate is $C$: since $C$ follows no one, every query ``does $C$ follow $l$?'' answers no, so $C$ is never replaced. Whenever $C$ itself is queried as $l$ (i.e.\ the current candidate $k\ne C$ is asked about $C$): since everyone follows $C$, the query ``does $k$ follow $C$?'' answers yes, so $C$ becomes the new candidate at that point. Either way, once $C$ has appeared as candidate or as $l$, it becomes (and remains) the candidate. Since every user appears as $l$ exactly once over the course of the sweep, $C$ is guaranteed to become the final candidate.

\[
\boxed{n-1\le n\text{ queries suffice}}
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Simulating elastic robot collisions in a corridor]
A corridor of length $L$ contains $n$ robots at distinct positions $x_1,\dots,x_n\in[0,L]$, each moving at unit speed in direction $+1$ or $-1$. On hitting a wall or another robot, a robot instantly reverses direction. After $t$ time units, (a) find the set of occupied positions (unordered, no repeats); (b) find each robot $i$'s own final position $y_i$. Give an efficient algorithm for both.
\end{example}
\begin{proof}[Solution]
\textbf{The ghost-particle trick.} Since all robots move at the same unit speed, two robots colliding and bouncing apart is kinematically indistinguishable, in terms of the set of positions occupied over time, from two identical ``ghost'' particles passing straight through each other with their identities swapped. So for computing raw positions, each robot can be treated as an independent particle that only reflects off the two walls, ignoring the others entirely.

\textbf{Single-particle wall reflection, in $O(1)$.} A particle starting at $x_i$ moving in direction $v_i=\pm1$ on the infinite line would be at $x_i+v_it$ at time $t$; the corridor motion (bouncing at $0$ and $L$) is obtained by folding this back into $[0,L]$ via the standard triangle-wave map: let $p=(x_i+v_it)\bmod 2L\in[0,2L)$, and the true position is $p$ if $p\le L$, or $2L-p$ if $p>L$. This models wall-bounces exactly and costs $O(1)$ per robot.

\textbf{(a) The occupied set.} Compute the $n$ ghost-positions via the $O(1)$ formula above ($O(n)$ total), then sort and remove duplicates ($O(n\log n)$) to get the position set (some ghost-positions may coincide, e.g.\ from two robots meeting head-on).

\[
\boxed{O(n\log n)\text{ time for the position set}}
\]

\textbf{(b) Each robot's own position.} Since real robots bounce apart rather than crossing, their left-to-right \emph{order} along the corridor is preserved for all time — no real robot ever passes another. So: sort the $n$ ghost-positions at time $t$ ($O(n\log n)$), and assign them, in increasing order, to the robots sorted by their \emph{initial} positions $x_1,\dots,x_n$ (also $O(n\log n)$ to sort once, up front): the robot that started $k$-th from the left is at the $k$-th smallest ghost-position at time $t$.

\[
\boxed{O(n\log n)\text{ time for every robot's own final position}}
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{itemize}
    \item Memorize formulas only after you understand where they come from.
    \item When a problem looks hard, first classify it: parity, conditioning, asymptotics, optimization, or linear structure.
    \item In an interview, write down the state variables explicitly. A correct state definition is usually half of the solution.
    \item For revision, cover the solutions and try to reproduce them from the definitions and formulas alone.
\end{itemize}

\begin{example}[Maximum subarray sum in $O(n)$ time and $O(1)$ extra memory]
Given an array of $n$ (possibly negative) numbers, design an algorithm to find the maximum possible sum of a contiguous subarray, using $O(n)$ time and $O(1)$ extra memory.
\end{example}
\begin{proof}[Solution]
This is Kadane's algorithm. Maintain two running values: $\mathrm{cur}$, the maximum sum of a subarray \emph{ending exactly at} the current index, and $\mathrm{best}$, the maximum seen over all indices so far.

\textbf{Recursion for $\mathrm{cur}$.} A subarray ending at index $i$ either consists of just $a[i]$ alone, or extends some subarray ending at $i-1$ by one more element. So the best sum ending at $i$ is $\mathrm{cur}_i=\max(a[i],\,\mathrm{cur}_{i-1}+a[i])$ — extend the previous best run if it's still helping (i.e.\ $\mathrm{cur}_{i-1}>0$), otherwise restart fresh at $a[i]$.

\textbf{Algorithm.} Initialize $\mathrm{cur}=\mathrm{best}=a[1]$. For $i=2,\dots,n$: set $\mathrm{cur}\leftarrow\max(a[i],\mathrm{cur}+a[i])$, then $\mathrm{best}\leftarrow\max(\mathrm{best},\mathrm{cur})$.

\textbf{Correctness.} By induction, $\mathrm{cur}$ after processing index $i$ equals the true maximum sum of a subarray ending exactly at $i$ (base case $i=1$ trivial; the recursion above is exactly right for extending). Since every subarray ends at some index, the overall maximum subarray sum is $\max_i(\text{best sum ending at }i)$, which is exactly what $\mathrm{best}$ tracks.

\[
\boxed{O(n)\text{ time},\ O(1)\text{ extra memory}}
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Can $20$ weighings order $10$ coins of different weights?]
There are $10$ coins of distinct weights, and a balance that, given any two coins, reports which is heavier. Can the full order of all $10$ coins by weight always be determined using at most $20$ weighings?
\end{example}
\begin{proof}[Solution]
\textbf{No.} Any correct comparison-based algorithm for determining a total order can be modeled as a binary decision tree: each internal node is one weighing (two possible outcomes), and each leaf corresponds to a single determined ordering (permutation) of the $10$ coins. Since the algorithm must be able to correctly report \emph{any} of the $10!$ possible true weight-orderings, and two different true orderings that produce the exact same sequence of weighing outcomes could never be told apart, every one of the $10!$ orderings must correspond to at least one distinct leaf of the tree.

A binary tree of depth $d$ has at most $2^d$ leaves, so a correct algorithm needs depth $d$ with $2^d\ge10!=3{,}628{,}800$. Since $2^{21}=2{,}097{,}152<10!$ and $2^{22}=4{,}194{,}304\ge10!$, the minimum possible depth is $d=22$. This means some sequence of true weights forces the algorithm down a root-to-leaf path of length $22$ — i.e.\ in the worst case, at least $22$ weighings are required.

\[
\boxed{\text{No: }20\text{ weighings are not always enough — the worst case needs at least }22}
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Longest balanced $0/1$ subarray in $O(n)$ time and space]
Given an array of $n$ zeros and ones, find the longest subarray containing an equal number of zeros and ones, in $O(n)$ time and $O(n)$ extra memory.
\end{example}
\begin{proof}[Solution]
\textbf{Reduction to prefix sums.} Replace each $0$ with $-1$, leaving the $1$'s unchanged, and let $S_0=0$, $S_i=S_{i-1}+a_i$ for $i=1,\dots,n$ be the running sum. A subarray from index $l+1$ to $r$ has equal counts of $0$'s and $1$'s if and only if $S_r=S_l$ (the $\pm1$ contributions cancel exactly).

\textbf{Algorithm.} Since each $a_i\in\{-1,1\}$, $S_i\in[-n,n]$, so allocate an array $\mathrm{first}[\,\cdot\,]$ indexed by this range ($O(n)$ space), initialized to ``unset.'' Scan $i=0,\dots,n$ in order: if $\mathrm{first}[S_i]$ is unset, record $\mathrm{first}[S_i]=i$ (the earliest index reaching this running sum); otherwise, this is a repeat, and the subarray from $\mathrm{first}[S_i]+1$ to $i$ is balanced — update the best answer with length $i-\mathrm{first}[S_i]$ if it's larger.

\textbf{Correctness.} For each value $v$ attained by $S$, the earliest index $l$ with $S_l=v$ paired with the \emph{latest} index $r$ with $S_r=v$ gives the longest balanced subarray with that particular running-sum value $v$ (since $S_l=S_r=v$ for any pair of indices with that value, and the longest such gap uses the extreme occurrences); processing indices left to right and always comparing against the first (earliest) occurrence, while continuously updating the best-so-far length, correctly finds this maximum length for every $v$, and hence overall.

\[
\boxed{O(n)\text{ time (one pass)},\ O(n)\text{ extra memory (the }\mathrm{first}[\,\cdot\,]\text{ array)}}
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Searching a monotone matrix needs at least $n$ comparisons]
Call an $n\times n$ array of reals $A$ \emph{monotone} if $A[k][l]\ge A[i][j]$ whenever $i\le k$ and $j\le l$. Given such an $A$ and a target $X$, an algorithm must decide whether $X$ occurs in $A$, using only comparisons of $A[i][j]$ against $X$. Prove no algorithm can guarantee an answer in fewer than $n$ comparisons.
\end{example}
\begin{proof}[Solution]
\textbf{The hard set: the anti-diagonal.} Consider the $n$ cells $D=\{(k,n{+}1{-}k):k=1,\dots,n\}$. For $k\ne k'$, neither $(k,n{+}1{-}k)$ nor $(k',n{+}1{-}k')$ dominates the other (one has a larger row index and smaller column index than the other), so monotonicity imposes \emph{no} constraint between any two cells of $D$ — their values could a priori be in any relative order.

\textbf{An adversary that never reveals information about $D$ for free.} The adversary answers each query $A[i][j]\lessgtr X$ on the fly, maintaining a set of numeric constraints consistent with monotonicity, as follows.
\begin{itemize}
\item If $(i,j)\in D$ itself, answer $A[i][j]\ne X$ (pick either direction) — this resolves exactly that one cell of $D$.
\item If $(i,j)\notin D$ with $i+j<n+1$: such a cell is \emph{dominated by} every $(k,n{+}1{-}k)\in D$ with $k\in[i,\,n{+}1{-}j]$ (a nonempty range), meaning those cells of $D$ are $\ge A[i][j]$. Answer $A[i][j]<X$ — this says nothing about whether the dominating (still larger) cells of $D$ are above or below $X$.
\item If $(i,j)\notin D$ with $i+j>n+1$: symmetrically, such a cell \emph{dominates} some cells of $D$ (which are therefore $\le A[i][j]$). Answer $A[i][j]>X$ — again uninformative about those dominated $D$-cells.
\end{itemize}
In every off-diagonal case the adversary's answer is consistent with \emph{any} eventual relationship between $X$ and the not-yet-queried cells of $D$ (a cell of $D$ bounded only from below by something $<X$, or only from above by something $>X$, can still be assigned any value, including exactly $X$, later). So off-diagonal queries never resolve any cell of $D$; only directly querying a cell of $D$ resolves it (and resolves only that one).

\textbf{Forcing $n$ queries.} Suppose the algorithm halts after fewer than $n$ total queries. Then some cell $(k_0,n{+}1{-}k_0)\in D$ was never queried, and by the above, its value is still unconstrained relative to $X$ (consistent bounds on it never pinned it down, by construction). The adversary can now retroactively complete the matrix two different ways, both consistent with every answer already given: set that cell to exactly $X$ (making $X$ present), or to some nearby value avoiding $X$ (making $X$ absent, since no other cell was ever forced to equal $X$ either). A correct algorithm must give different final answers on these two completions, yet it observed identical query results in both — contradiction.

\[
\boxed{\text{at least }n\text{ comparisons are always necessary}}
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Zeroing all bad rows and columns of a bit matrix in $O(1)$ extra memory]
An $n\times n$ matrix of $0/1$ bits is given (one bit of storage per entry). Call a row or column \emph{bad} if it contains at least one $0$. Design an algorithm that zeroes out every bad row and column, using $O(1)$ extra memory, and analyze its running time.
\end{example}
\begin{proof}[Solution]
\textbf{Step 1: record the fate of row $1$ and column $1$.} Scan row $1$ and column $1$ ($O(n)$ time) and store, in $2$ extra bits, whether each was originally bad (contained a $0$) or good (all $1$'s).

\textbf{Step 2: use row $1$/column $1$ as bad-markers for everything else.} For each $i,j=2,\dots,n$: if $a_{ij}=0$, set $a_{i1}\leftarrow0$ and $a_{1j}\leftarrow0$. After this pass ($O(n^2)$ time, no extra memory), $a_{i1}=0$ exactly when row $i$ (for $i\ge2$) is bad, and $a_{1j}=0$ exactly when column $j$ (for $j\ge2$) is bad — row $1$ and column $1$ now double as a lookup table for everyone else's status.

\textbf{Step 3: apply the markers.} For each $i,j=2,\dots,n$: if $a_{i1}=0$ or $a_{1j}=0$, set $a_{ij}\leftarrow0$. This correctly zeroes every bad row and column, for every index $\ge2$ ($O(n^2)$ time).

\textbf{Step 4: fix up row $1$/column $1$ themselves.} Using the $2$ bits saved in Step 1, if row $1$ was originally bad, zero it out now; likewise for column $1$ ($O(n)$ time).

\[
\boxed{O(n^2)\text{ time},\ O(1)\text{ extra memory}}
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Finding a valid starting gas station on a circular route]
A circular track has gas stations at known positions, each stocked with a known amount of fuel; a car with known, constant fuel consumption starts with an empty tank. Design an $O(n)$-time algorithm that finds a starting station from which the car can complete the entire loop (refueling at each station reached), or determines none exists.
\end{example}
\begin{proof}[Solution]
\textbf{Setup.} Number the stations $1,\dots,n$ in track order, and let $g[i]$ be the net fuel change from refueling at station $i$ and then driving to station $i+1$ (fuel gained minus fuel consumed over that leg). Starting at station $s$, the tank level after visiting station $i$ (going around, wrapping indices mod $n$) is a running sum of $g$-values starting from $s$; the route is completable from $s$ iff every such running sum (partial sum of $g$, cyclically starting at $s$) stays $\ge0$.

\textbf{No solution if the total is negative.} Let $\mathrm{Total}=\sum_{i=1}^ng[i]$. If $\mathrm{Total}<0$, the tank's level after a full loop from any start is $\mathrm{Total}<0$, so completing the loop (which requires every partial sum $\ge0$, in particular the full-loop sum) is impossible from any starting station.

\textbf{Otherwise, start right after the minimum prefix sum.} Assume $\mathrm{Total}\ge0$. Let $h[k]=\sum_{i=1}^kg[i]$ (with $h[0]=0$), and let $j\in\{0,\dots,n\}$ minimize $h[j]$. Claim: starting at station $j+1$ (indices mod $n$) works.

Re-index so station $1$ is the old station $j+1$; write $h'[k]$ for the running sum under this new indexing. For $k\le n-j$ (before wrapping past the old end of the array), $h'[k]=h[j+k]-h[j]\ge0$ since $h[j]$ is the global minimum of $h$, so $h[j+k]\ge h[j]$. For $k>n-j$ (after wrapping around), writing $k'=k-(n-j)\in\{1,\dots,j\}$,
\[
h'[k]=\bigl(\mathrm{Total}-h[j]\bigr)+h[k']\ge\bigl(\mathrm{Total}-h[k']\bigr)+h[k']=\mathrm{Total}\ge0,
\]
using $h[j]\le h[k']$ (again, $h[j]$ is the global minimum). So every running sum under the new indexing is $\ge0$: the route is completable starting at station $j+1$.

\textbf{Complexity.} Computing all prefix sums $h[0],\dots,h[n]$ and locating their minimum is a single linear scan.
\[
\boxed{O(n)\text{ time}}
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Finding the combined median of two sorted groups in $11$ weighings]
There are $2012$ weights of distinct masses, split into two groups of $1006$, each internally sorted by mass (but the relative order between groups unknown). Using only pairwise weighings (comparisons), find the $1006$th-lightest weight overall in at most $11$ weighings.
\end{example}
\begin{proof}[Solution]
\textbf{Goal, restated.} We want the median (lower of the two central ranks, here exactly rank $1006$ out of $2012$) of the union of two sorted lists $A[1..1006]$, $B[1..1006]$.

\textbf{Invariant.} Maintain candidate windows $A'\subseteq A$, $B'\subseteq B$ of equal current size $s$ (starting at $s=1006$), with the guarantee that the target is exactly the $s$-th smallest element of $A'\cup B'$ (initially true by hypothesis, since $s=1006$ is exactly the target rank over the full $2s=2012$ elements).

\textbf{One round.} Compare the middle elements $A'[m]$ and $B'[m]$, $m=\lceil s/2\rceil$. Say $A'[m]\le B'[m]$ (the other case is symmetric). Then: every element of $B'$ from $B'[m]$ up to $B'[s]$ (there are $s-m+1\ge\lceil s/2\rceil$ of them) has at least $m$ elements of $A'$ and $m$ elements of $B'$ below or equal to it, i.e.\ rank $\ge s+1$ within $A'\cup B'$ when $s$ is odd (and $\ge s$, requiring a tie-break, when $s$ is even) — either way these can be discarded from the top of $B'$ as definitely not the target. Discard the same number, $\lfloor s/2\rfloor$, from the bottom of $A'$ (symmetric argument: they have enough elements above them to be definitely below the target rank), decreasing the target rank by exactly $\lfloor s/2\rfloor$ to match the shrunken universe. Both windows shrink from $s$ to $s-\lfloor s/2\rfloor=\lceil s/2\rceil$, and the invariant (target = new window size) is preserved. This uses exactly $1$ comparison per round.

\textbf{Counting rounds.} Starting from $s=1006$ and applying $s\mapsto\lceil s/2\rceil$:
\[
1006\to503\to252\to126\to63\to32\to16\to8\to4\to2\to1,
\]
exactly $10$ steps to reach windows of size $1$ in each group.

\textbf{Final step.} With $s=1$, the invariant says the target is the smaller of the two remaining single weights — one final ($11$th) comparison identifies it.

\[
\boxed{10\text{ halving rounds}+1\text{ final comparison}=11\text{ weighings}}
\]
\end{proof}

\end{document}
